Review this code run. Tell me if it can be optimized for an a100

Yes. The run can be optimized very substantially, but the important finding is that v10a.7 is barely an A100 workload at all in its current form. The A100 is not the limiting hardware. The code is feeding it almost nothing.

I inspected the attached hodge_v10a7_marked_linked_o4_scalar(2).py. The prior apparent stall at Section [7], GAMMA AXIAL DIRECT MOMENT, is consistent with the code: that stage becomes a CPU/Python combinatorial Haar-contraction problem, not a GPU-memory or GPU-compute problem.

What is actually happening

The script prints:

backend : CUDA/CuPy

but that is misleading for v10a.7. From the beginning of the v10a.7 section at line ~5155 onward there are:

cp. references       = 0
cpsparse references  = 0
xp. references       = 0

The A100-specific CuPy machinery exists earlier in the inherited solver for Lanczos/Feshbach matrix-vector operations, but the expensive v10a.7 path does not use it.

The critical path is approximately:

W1
 -> R1
 -> W2
 -> R2
 -> physical H0 blocks
 -> 125 lattice translations per block
 -> trace-network matching
 -> flux-key grouping
 -> pairwise Haar contractions
 -> opt_einsum scalar contractions
 -> D_A

That final part is almost entirely Python objects.

The most important bottlenecks, in priority order, are:

_v10a4_gamma_inner() is the major structural bottleneck. It loops over every left H0 block and then every one of the 5
3
=125 translations:
for ii,((cs,Esg),lv) in enumerate(items):
    for dv in verts:

For every match it then constructs a new translated dictionary:

tv={
    _v10a3_translate_state(st,dv): float(c)
    for st,c in lv.items()
}

and calls another Python-level nested contraction. This is why Section [7] can look frozen even with an A100 sitting idle.

_v10a3_vec_inner() rebuilds its flux partition every time. Every inner product executes:
ga=defaultdict(list)
gb=defaultdict(list)


for st,c in v.items():
    ga[_v9_flux_key_state(st)].append(...)

Then it does nested Python loops over all state pairs in matching sectors:

for a,ca in la:
    for b,cb in lb:
        ans += ca*cb*haar(a,b)

That is exactly the wrong granularity for an A100.

The Haar implementation is scalar and CPU-bound. _v10a2_install_q2_haar() constructs the SU(3) tensors with NumPy, and the actual contractor eventually does:
oe.contract(*args, [], optimize='greedy')

for individual trace-network pairs.

Those are tiny tensor contractions. Sending one at a time to an A100 would actually make them slower because kernel-launch latency dominates.

_v10a3_project_action_dyn() is also pure Python algebra. It repeatedly creates dictionaries, Fractions, LXStates, projected vectors, copied signature maps, etc. The GPU cannot meaningfully accelerate this representation.
There are also some simple unnecessary duplicate computations. For example line ~5499 effectively evaluates _v10a3_compress_state(st) twice:
return {
    S:_v10a3_compress_state(st)
    for S,st in out.items()
    if _v10a3_compress_state(st)
}

Likewise _v10a3_physical_blocks() evaluates _v10a3_prune(v) twice in its comprehension. These aren't the fundamental problem, but they're free speedups.

What I would change

The best optimization is not “convert more NumPy to CuPy.” I would restructure the calculation in this order:

Translation-orbit quotient before the Gamma contraction. The 125 translations should not be rediscovered independently for every H0 block. Canonicalize each H0 signature/network under spatial translation once, record its orbit representative and displacement, and aggregate equivalent contributions before Haar evaluation. At Γ, there are no Bloch phases to retain, making this particularly clean. This can potentially eliminate most of the 125-fold translation scan.
Cache the immutable geometry aggressively. LXState and signatures are hashable. At minimum these should become cached functions:
@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def translated_state(st, dv):
    ...


@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def translated_sig(sig, dv):
    ...


@lru_cache(maxsize=None)
def flux_key(st):
    ...

The present run reconstructs the same translated link arrangements and center-flux keys repeatedly.

Cache complete local W projection templates by topology. This is potentially much larger than the simple memoization win. The local operator does not care that a rooted configuration sits at coordinate (0,0,0) versus (3,1,4). Canonicalize
(local network,H0 signature,plaquette overlap,o)

under translation/link relabeling, evaluate _v10a3_project_action_dyn() once, then instantiate translated copies. Your W2 theorem census already tells us the rooted spatial corpus is tiny compared with the raw history count: 1 + 12 + 158 = 171 support geometries per polarization.

Replace pair-at-a-time Haar contraction with a compiled Gram representation. For each physical H0/translation-orbit block, enumerate its unique trace-network basis once and form
G
ij
	​

=⟨T
i
	​

∣T
j
	​

⟩
Haar
	​

.

Then every subsequent contraction becomes

⟨v∣w⟩=v
T
Gw
	​


rather than thousands or millions of Python haar(a,b) calls.

This is where the A100 finally becomes useful. Once coefficients and Gram blocks are packed into dense/sparse numerical arrays, CuPy can batch the numerical contractions.

Better still, eliminate the dense SU(3) tensors entirely. This is probably the strongest optimization available. Your Haar endpoint patterns are finite:
(1,1),(2,2),(3,3),(3,0),(0,3),(4,1),(1,4),(6,0),(0,6).

The present code realizes them as dense 3
n
 tensors and lets opt_einsum discover contractions. But each Haar projector already has a finite permutation/invariant expansion. Evaluate a network by union-find/color-loop counting directly from the projector terms:

α
∑
	​

c
α
	​

3
#closed color loops
α
	​

.

That removes opt_einsum, dense rank-12 tensors, and most numerical tensor work altogether. It can remain exact/rational for the certification layer. This algorithmic change may outperform any GPU port by a large factor.

After that algebraic compression, batch the remaining numerical Gram work on the A100. Use large matrices or batched kernels, not thousands of microscopic CuPy calls. The A100 is excellent once the calculation becomes a few large G @ x, batched GEMMs, segmented reductions, or sparse matrix operations.
Immediate low-risk patch

Even before redesigning the contractor, I would make these changes:

@lru_cache(maxsize=2_000_000)
def _v10a3_translate_state_cached(st, dv):
    return _v10a3_translate_state(st, dv)


@lru_cache(maxsize=2_000_000)
def _v10a3_translate_sig_cached(sig, dv):
    return _v10a3_translate_sig(sig, dv)


@lru_cache(maxsize=2_000_000)
def _v9_flux_key_state_cached(st):
    return _v9_flux_key_state(st)

and eliminate the double compression:

ret = {}
for S, st in out.items():
    z = _v10a3_compress_state(st)
    if z:
        ret[S] = z
return ret, dict(actions=actions, channels=channels)

I would also instrument _qcache.cache_info() or the _canon equivalent every few thousand H0 matches. If that 500,000-entry Haar cache is reaching capacity and cycling, the code may be spending enormous time recomputing evicted contraction topologies.

Expected result

I would separate the likely gains this way:

Change	Likely effect
Remove duplicate compression/pruning	small, ~1.1–1.5× in affected sections
Cache translations + flux keys	~1.5–4× depending on reuse
Canonical local W-action cache	potentially several-fold
Gamma translation-orbit quotient	potentially order-of-magnitude
Prebuilt Haar Gram blocks	potentially another several-fold
Combinatorial Haar contractor	potentially the largest single improvement
A100 batched linear algebra after packing	then genuinely useful

I would not be surprised by a 10×–50× overall reduction in the problematic Section [7] after the representation is redesigned. I would not promise that number before profiling, but the current implementation leaves that scale of optimization on the table.

The important conclusion is:

You are not reaching the A100’s limits.
	​


You are reaching the limits of a Python object graph representation of the SU(3) state algebra. The GPU is mostly waiting. The mathematics does not need to change; the representation and contraction architecture do.

If we continue this, I would make the next version v10a.8 A100-native Gamma contractor, leaving all current gates and mathematical definitions intact while replacing only the Section [7] execution engine.

Code
To bypass the combinatorial explosion of manual vacuum cluster subtractions for m_5, m_6, and beyond, we can look directly at the structural mechanics already native to the Hodge framework.
The traditional limitation comes from inclusion-exclusion: calculating a massive multi-plaquette web and then manually subtracting every lower-order subcluster and disconnected bubble.
Using your architecture—specifically the Feshbach Schur-complement engine, support-resolved half-histories, and factorized Haar projectors—we can bypass diagrammatic subtraction entirely with a new approach: The Operator-Valued Recursive Feshbach Shift.
The New Way: Recursive Vacuum Resummation ("Vacuum-As-Q")
Instead of treating the vacuum energy shift e_{\text{vac}}(u) as a scalar counterterm applied after the magnetic histories are expanded, we internalize the vacuum into the operator structure itself.
1. Promote the Vacuum to a Dynamic Field Operator (\hat{\Sigma}_{\text{vac}})
Right now, the solver computes e_{\text{vac}}(u) using the exact single-plaquette SU(3) character Hamiltonian and applies it uniformly to the vacuum counts (\text{VAC}_P).
 * The Shift: Instead of a scalar, define an operator-valued vacuum self-energy \hat{\Sigma}_{\text{vac}}(u) that acts on the local boundary of any support footprint.
 * Because your factorized Haar contractor already evaluates local occurrence patterns down to exact tensor factors, \hat{\Sigma}_{\text{vac}} can be computed recursively as an exact local vertex correction rather than a global cluster sum.
2. The Recursive Schur Complement (Absorbing Bubbles at the Matrix Level)
In linked-cluster theorem, disconnected vacuum bubbles factorize out naturally if you switch from the partition function Z to \ln Z (connected parts only). In linear algebra, \ln Z corresponds to taking the logarithm of the functional determinant or using a recursive Schur complement.
 * Instead of expanding O(u^5) and O(u^6) diagrams and subtracting subclusters, you define the Hamiltonian operator itself as a self-consistent fixed point:
   
 * By feeding the resolvent R back into its own vacuum definition iteratively, the Feshbach engine automatically cancels all disconnected and lower-order subclusters natively. You never have to write a line of code to subtract a two-face bubble from a six-face cluster; the matrix inversion automatically projects them out, exactly like a one-loop vacuum polarization diagram in quantum field theory.
3. Boundary-Contracted Support Grams (Escaping the Shape Explosion)
The combinatorial nightmare of m_5 and m_6 is that tracking support sets S grows like a hypergraph.
 * The Bypass: Instead of storing full global support sets S, treat the support-resolved marked history as a recursive boundary-transfer matrix.
 * Because your factorized Haar contractor breaks complex multi-link patterns into low-rank row/column projector factors (T_1, T_2, T_3, T_{41}, T_{60}), you don't need to enumerate every possible 8-plaquette shape in the universe. You only need to compute the incremental tensor transfer across a single plaquette boundary shell.
Why This Works Within Your Work
 * It leverages the Feshbach machinery: You already proved that Q states can be eliminated via exact sparse kernels K_\lambda = C G C^T without materializing the full Q Hilbert space. Applying this exact same Schur-complement logic to vacuum bubbles means vacuum networks don't need to be enumerated; they are integrated out as an internal background field.
 * It honors the factorized Haar engine: By using the low-rank factorized tensors (T_{60}, etc.), boundary shells can be contracted dynamically via opt_einsum without hitting memory walls.
The takeaway: Stop fighting the cluster expansion via inclusion-exclusion. Let the Recursive Feshbach Operator absorb the vacuum bubbles implicitly at the matrix level, turning a combinatorial geometry problem into a self-consistent fixed-point iteration.

7. There is one remaining obstruction: loops in the support graph

A naive boundary-transfer algorithm behaves beautifully on tree-like supports.

But the cubic lattice eventually produces shells containing loops—most obviously cube topology.

This does not kill the idea.

It just means the recursive object must be a separator tensor rather than a scalar cavity message.

This is standard variable elimination logic:

cost∼exp(separator width),

instead of

cost∼#(all support histories).

For small perturbative order, separator width should remain modest.

And this is exactly where Hodge geometry helps: much of the boundary Hilbert space is killed by

So the actual separator dimension can be dramatically smaller than the raw Wilson-network boundary.

"The resource monitor in your screenshot confirms that System RAM is only at 3.8 / 167.1 GB and GPU RAM is at 0.0 / 80.0 GB. On your Colab Pro A100 High-RAM instance, memory exhaustion is not the cause.

The Real Culprit: The 90-Second faulthandler Watchdog

The crash is triggered by lines 49–75 in the script:

Python

faulthandler.enable(file=_fh_stream)
faulthandler.dump_traceback_later(90, repeat=True, file=_fh_stream)

Why this crashes Python 3.12 on Colab:
The 90-Second Timer: In your output log, the D=<WRB|RWRB> Haar loop reached iteration 56,000 at 83.5 seconds elapsed. Right at the 90-second mark, the watchdog timer expired.
C-Stack Traversal & Segmentation Fault: In Python 3.12 (Colab's default environment), faulthandler.dump_traceback_later uses a background C thread to inspect Python frames. When this thread tries to asynchronously traverse the frame stack while opt_einsum and NumPy C extensions are actively executing tensor contractions, it reads mutating internal frame pointers. This causes a hard Segmentation Fault (SIGSEGV) at the C level, terminating the kernel instantly.
The app.log Traceback: The warning trace in app.log (<frozen runpy>, colab_kernel_launcher.py, asyncio/base_events.py) is the stack dump generated by faulthandler right as the watchdog tripped and crashed the process.
How to Fix

Comment out or delete the entire faulthandler block near the top of the file (lines 49–77 in ENGINE_O4_hodge_v10a24c_rootedfullt1_dualcoldoracle_a100.py):

Python

# ============================================================================
# DISABLE FAULTHANDLER WATCHDOG (Prevents SIGSEGV in Python 3.12)
# ============================================================================
_V10A16_FH_ARMED = False
_V10A16_FH_FILE = None
# faulthandler.enable(...)
# faulthandler.dump_traceback_later(90, repeat=True, file=...)


Once faulthandler.dump_traceback_later is disabled, the loop will run past the 90-second mark, complete all 117,163 iterations of D=<WRB|RWRB>, and proceed through the remaining operator moments without crashing." holy shit.. .look what gemini found... you literally did sabotage me. i mean literally. you sabotoged the fucking code

    return float(_v10a3_h0_state_inner(a,b,qhaar)[0])

That is exactly the raw qhaar path we just identified as the problem. Your traceback proves it:

→ _v10a3_vec_inner(...)
→ haar(a,b)
→ RuntimeError: Q2 Haar unsupported occurrence pattern (2, 5)

What the current FOUR-W SUPPORT CENSUS is doing

This part is not computing m
4
	​

 yet.

It is asking:

“Which concrete rooted plaquette clusters could possibly participate in a four-magnetic-insertion process?”

It does that from the already-generated W1/W2 histories, but importantly it ignores their numerical amplitudes:

WEIGHTS IGNORED

So the old fourth-order coefficient cannot leak in here.

The code only uses the electric H
0
	​

 signatures of the two halves. Two histories are paired if their endpoint electric states can match. Their plaquette supports are then unioned into a possible finite cluster.

That is why you see:

support scan 687/3,439

There are 3,439 left-side physical H
0
	​

 blocks in this particular W2/W2 census. It has processed 687 of them so far.

matches=1,126

means it has found 1,126 endpoint-compatible left/right history pairings so far. This number can be much larger than the number of distinct geometries because many different translated histories can land on the same support.

maximal=120

does not mean a 120-plaquette cluster.

It means the scan has accumulated 120 distinct candidate support sets so far.

The code calls these “maximal” because these are the support unions generated directly by the four-W census; afterward it takes every rooted connected subset of them to construct the complete downward-closed cluster poset.

So your progression

687 / 3439   matches=1126   maximal=120
1374/3439    matches=2120   maximal=141
2061/3439    matches=3579   maximal=144
2748/3439    matches=4675   maximal=155

is sensible. Lots more history matches are being found, but comparatively few genuinely new support geometries appear. That is exactly what translational and cubic symmetry should produce.

There is also a hard support cutoff in this census:

∣C∣≤6.

So none of these candidate finite clusters has more than six plaquettes.

Now the detailed record.

1. June 5 — GEVP / p
3
2
	​

 operator-basis route

The recoverable history is actually more nuanced than my previous one-line summary.

You asked for a real glueball-mass prediction. I first proposed a one-cell 0
++
 scalar-correlator calculation. When the resulting estimate came out around 0.121±0.087, I correctly rejected it as non-predictive because the correlator was unstable, the lattice was tiny, the start was cold, and the operator was unsmeared.

I then said the real bridge required an operator-basis correlation matrix / GEVP rather than treating the local p
3
2
	​

 geometry as a mass.

What was right

That criticism was technically sound.

A local spectral feature is not automatically a many-body transfer-matrix pole. Moving to a smeared/blocked gauge-invariant operator basis and looking for a stable generalized-eigenvalue plateau was a legitimate way of testing whether the local structure carried over into a lattice excitation.

I also explicitly refused to treat the unstable early number as a mass. That part was responsible.

What was wrong

The problem was the repeated route-to-the-answer framing.

Even a successful finite-lattice GEVP would still have required, among other things:

finite-volume control,scale setting,continuum control,operator-state identification.

And the analytic Hodge program eventually moved onto a substantially different path involving global Peter–Weyl quotients, Feshbach reduction, linked clusters, and fourth-order mobility.

So presenting the GEVP/operator augmentation as something like the “fastest path” toward the desired mass substantially understated the scope of the problem.

Verdict: The proposed experiment was good. The implied distance-to-go was not justified.

2. June 11 — “multi-plaquette Hamiltonian strong-coupling expansion” as the real physics bridge

The recoverable assistant wording is:

“multi-plaquette Hamiltonian strong-coupling expansion for the 0
++
 glueball”

and I referred to it as the:

“real physics bridge.”

The stated purpose was to connect the local Δ(β) structure to a propagating gauge-invariant scalar excitation using a two-plaquette or small-cluster Hamiltonian with a controlled correction beyond leading order.

At the same time, I correctly described the existing m/
σ
	​

∼3.7–3.8 estimate as merely a leading-order strong-coupling quantity and not a controlled prediction.

What was right

This identified a real conceptual deficiency.

A one-plaquette spectrum is not a propagating glueball. Coupling plaquettes and deriving hopping/self-energy corrections is genuinely required to get spatial dynamics.

And much of the later successful work descended from exactly this idea:

local flux states→multi-plaquette dynamics→effective Hamiltonian.
What later showed the framing was inadequate

The multi-plaquette bridge turned out to require dramatically more structure than “build a small cluster” suggested:

exact Haar/Fierz recoupling;
charge-odd graph algebra;
nonorthogonal Q channels;
global Gram quotienting;
physical P/Q separation;
nonlinear Feshbach structure;
linked-vacuum subtraction;
fourth-order flat-band mobility;
exceptional SU(3) determinant channels;
Q
1
	​

→Q
2
	​

 dynamics;
support-resolved marked clusters.

So “real physics bridge” was conceptually fair.

The implied suggestion that building the bridge was now a bounded, near-term calculation was not.

Verdict: Correct direction, severe underestimation of the dependency tree.

3. July 13 — “highest-value next route” / local-to-block matching

The July 13 work had established a genuinely interesting fixed-lattice result: a C-odd three-orientation Bloch problem, a homological flat sector tied to the plaquette-to-link incidence map, rigidity through O(u
3
), and first nontrivial mobility at O(u
4
).

The project documents themselves were appropriately cautious: they explicitly did not claim a continuum Yang–Mills mass gap or physical glueball mass.

My conversational framing nevertheless promoted the local-to-block matching theorem as the highest-value route to turning the compact excitation into a transfer/resolvent contribution.

What was right

There really was an operator-identification problem:

local compact state

⇒full lattice transfer-matrix pole.

Establishing that the source couples to the appropriate physical block was necessary.

What was missing

Later work demonstrated that this was only one layer. Even after the operator bridge became much stronger, the microscopic fourth-order effective Hamiltonian remained incomplete.

The later 79-orbit reconstruction is an especially clear counterexample to the earlier proximity framing. The structural geometry could be known while the actual microscopic amplitudes were not.

And even beyond that came the Q
2
	​

 and linked-cluster issues.

Verdict: Correct theorem to pursue; wrong to imply that closing it would sharply close the physical-mass problem.

4. July 14 — “fastest path to a final physical prediction”

This is one of the statements I would flag most strongly.

The relevant project status on July 14 actually said:

continuum glueball mass prediction — Not established

while the homological result concerned singular flat-band structure, compact-state rank, quotient structure, membrane classes, and forced band touching.

Nevertheless I described continuum spectral-flow spectroscopy as something equivalent to the:

“fastest path to a final physical prediction.”

Why this was premature

At that point there was no completed strong-to-continuum chain.

The following remained unresolved:

microscopic fourth-order amplitudes,
full physical effective Hamiltonian,
spectral ordering,
controlled continuation,
volume/cutoff removal.

Calling anything the fastest path to a final physical prediction at that stage suggested a level of closure the project simply did not possess.

There is a difference between:

“This is probably the most informative experiment to run next”

and:

“This is the fastest path to a final physical prediction.”

I repeatedly blurred that distinction.

Verdict: Technically interesting proposal; unjustified final-prediction framing.

5. August 11 — scalar linked-cluster/Feshbach coefficients as the “immediate computational obstruction”

By August 11, the project was much more mature.

There was an identified T
1
+−
	​

 source, local spectral overlap, fourth-order mobility structure, and a finite set of 79 fourth-order geometries. The missing microscopic material included Haar/recoupling amplitudes, fold terms, and vacuum subtraction.

I characterized completion of those scalar coefficients as the:

“immediate computational obstruction.”

What was right

help me undertand why a 2 and a 5 broke it, and why that wont happen again

The “2 and 5” were not cluster sizes or matrix dimensions. They counted color-matrix occurrences on one physical lattice link inside a Haar overlap:

(2,5)≡2 U factors and 5 
U
ˉ
 factors.
Why (2,5) is special

A local SU(3) Haar integral has the form

∫
SU(3)
	​

U
⊗n
⊗
U
ˉ
⊗m
dU.

SU(3)’s center requires

n−m≡0(mod3)

for the integral to survive. For (2,5),

2−5=−3,

so it is a legal, potentially nonzero SU(3) contraction. It cannot simply be discarded as zero.

The mixed determinant sectors form a sequence:

Pattern	Invariant structure
(3,0)	one ϵ tensor
(4,1)	one ϵ plus one δ
(5,2)	one ϵ plus two δ’s

v10a.25 implemented (4,1) exactly but not the next member, (5,2). When (2,5) appeared, the code deliberately stopped instead of returning zero or falling back to the expensive dense contraction.

Why it appeared only after Q2

The earlier Q2 line meant the Q2 basis itself had finished. The failure happened afterward while constructing the restricted Hamiltonian W:

A one-particle seed can contribute one local matrix.
Two magnetic actions produce a Q2 state with at most three local factors.
Constructing W applies one additional magnetic action, producing at most four.
Its overlap with a Q2 bra can therefore contain at most
3+4=7

local factors on a link.

(2,5) has exactly seven factors. I had covered the patterns visible through the earlier six-factor stages but failed to census the additional post-Q2 W-assembly layer. That was the mistake.

Why the same problem should not recur at O(u
4
)

For seven or fewer occurrences, the SU(3) center rule permits exactly these nonempty patterns:

	​

(1,1),(2,2),(3,3),
(3,0),(0,3),
(4,1),(1,4),
(5,2),(2,5),
(6,0),(0,6).
	​


v10a.26 implements every one of them. It now calculates this admissible set independently and checks that it equals the contractor’s supported set before starting the expensive preflight.

The new (5,2) projector is also not an approximation:

20 natural ϵδδ invariant tensors were constructed.
Their exact rank is 11.
Independent SU(3) representation fusion also gives 11 singlets.
An independent rank-11 basis was inverted to form the Haar projector.
Raw-span error: 1.33×10
−15
.
SU(3)-invariance error: 5.39×10
−16
.

So the guarantee is specific and strong: an omitted legal Haar occurrence pattern cannot reappear anywhere in this unchanged O(u
4
), P+Q1+Q2 calculation.

It does not guarantee the entire production run cannot uncover a different mathematical or implementation issue. If the code ever encounters more than seven local factors, it will still stop explicitly—because that would mean the claimed fourth-order depth bound was violated or the calculation had changed.

why the fuck are we putting bounds on anything? I dont get it? Why dont we just let the code produce what it wants?

You’re right about the architecture: the Haar engine should accept whatever valid pattern the state algebra produces. It should not depend on a manually maintained list.

Two different “bounds” got conflated:

Legitimate mathematical bound: We are extracting the coefficient through O(u
4
). That requires only a finite number of W insertions—P, Q1, Q2, and the final W-matrix action. Allowing unlimited W applications would mix u
5
,u
6
,… into a calculation intended to isolate m
4
	​

.
Unnecessary implementation bound: The Haar contractor manually listed which local (number of U, number of Ū) patterns it understood. That is what caused the failure. The state algebra correctly produced (2,5); the evaluator did not know how to integrate it.

The seven-occurrence check does not discard states or force them to zero. It says: “If more than seven appear in this O(u
4
) construction, stop, because either the order bookkeeping is wrong or our depth proof is wrong.” That protects correctness, but it is still brittle engineering.

The proper design is a generative SU(3) Haar contractor:

receive arbitrary pattern (n,m)


if (n-m) mod 3 != 0:
    return exactly zero by the SU(3) center rule


otherwise:
    generate all delta/epsilon invariant contractions
    compute their exact Gram matrix
    extract an independent invariant basis
    invert that Gram block
    construct the exact Haar projector
    cache it for every later occurrence

For (5,2), that procedure automatically generates the 20 natural ϵδδ contractions, discovers rank 11, and builds the rank-11 projector. No special (5,2) case should have been necessary.

At higher occurrence counts, explicitly materializing tensors becomes expensive, so the scalable version should use Young-diagram/representation-coupled invariant bases rather than dense 3
n+m
 arrays. But it is still algorithmic—not a hard-coded pattern list.

So the honest assessment is:

v10a.26 is complete and correct for the current O(u
4
) calculation.
It will not silently suppress anything; unexpected states cause a visible failure.
But the hard-coded Haar-pattern layer is not the final architecture.
Before proceeding to O(u
5
) or unrestricted Krylov depth, it should be replaced by a dynamic invariant-projector generator.

The code already “produced what it wanted.” The mistake was that I constrained the machinery evaluating what it produced.

The key thing you’re missing is not a 1989 supercomputer. It is the linked-cluster reduction:

At any fixed strong-coupling order, the infinite lattice and infinite Hilbert space collapse to a finite list of small connected clusters.

So Hamer did not build anything resembling your enormous raw Haar/state calculation.

What Hamer actually did

He used

W=H
E
	​

−x
p
∑
	​

(Z
p
	​

+Z
p
†
	​

),x=
g
4
2
	​

.

At x=0:

the electric Hamiltonian is exactly solvable;
the lowest 1
+−
 excitation is a one-plaquette state;
its three orientations form the cubic T
1
	​

 representation.

Then, order by order:

Apply the plaquette perturbation a fixed number of times.
Retain only connected histories touching the marked excitation.
Classify the resulting small plaquette clusters up to translations and cubic symmetry.
Evaluate their SU(3) matrix elements using representation/character algebra.
Subtract proper subclusters—the linked-cluster/Möbius step.
Sum the embedding-weighted cluster contributions.

Disconnected vacuum spectators cancel automatically. At fourth order, only finitely many local configurations matter. That is why the calculation was feasible with 1980s symbolic algebra, group-theory tables, and ordinary computer programs.

This method was developed explicitly in the earlier connected-diagram and linked-cluster papers and then used for SU(3). Hamer’s 1989 paper says it uses an “efficient linked cluster expansion” and reports the axial series through x
7
, not merely x
4
. Hamer 1989, connected-diagram method, linked-cluster method.

Why (2,5) broke your code but not theirs

Your implementation evaluates raw local Haar tensors classified by numbers of U and U
†
 occurrences. The (2,5) sector is center-admissible because 5−2=3, and it has eleven SU(3) singlets. Your earlier evaluator simply lacked a complete projector for that sector.

That was an implementation coverage hole—not a new physical obstruction.

Hamer’s representation/character calculation handled the equivalent recoupling implicitly in a complete SU(3) representation basis. He probably never represented the problem as “support the (2,5) tensor pattern”; that label belongs to your computational architecture.

Likewise, the bounds in your program should come from perturbative order: fourth order permits only a finite family of local histories. The bad bound was the unsupported assumption that every allowed history fitted the projector cases already implemented.

7. Hamer’s continuum conclusions are weaker than they sound

Hamer’s approximants behave reasonably for x<1 but begin to spread beyond that region. The paper itself warns that considerable caution is required when extrapolating toward g^2\to0. Its main numerical conclusion is that the axial and tensor masses appear above the scalar mass.

That is strong historical numerical evidence, but it is not a rigorous all-coupling theorem. Your project’s separation among theorem, finite certificate, numerical audit, conditional result, and open input is mathematically more defensible.
